What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... it started out with me just trying to eat cleaner. I'm a firefighter, and I'm pushing for the lieutenant track right now. You really have to stand out and be in top shape, especially with the new recruits coming out of the academy. Man, these kids they hire lately, they look like they just live at a CrossFit gym but they barely know how to properly vent a roof... we had this one rookie last shift who completely jammed the K-12 saw, it was an absolute mess. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the lieutenant thing. I wanted to get leaner for it. But it's kind of turned into me just cutting my meals way, way down. Like, I'll basically just run on black coffee—which is brutal because the coffee maker at our station is basically brewing battery acid, we've been begging the captain to approve a new one for months—and maybe a protein shake, and then just a really small dinner. And that's even on shift days. I know I'm burning way more than that when we're actually out on calls, hauling all that gear around, but... yeah, I guess it just got a bit out of hand and my captain told me I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight with this new eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't know if I'm actively trying to lose a ton more right now, but I definitely can't gain any. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—say, after a big station meal or just from drinking extra water—I get this really tight panic. Like I've completely blown it and people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nWhich is tough because the guys at the station cook these massive, heavy meals all the time. Miller, this guy on C-shift, he makes this baked ziti that's basically just a solid block of ricotta and sausage. He says it's his grandmother's recipe, and he literally never shuts up about her. She just turned 90 and still lives in Brooklyn, I think? Or maybe Queens. She used to cook for their whole neighborhood back in the day, so he cooks like he's feeding an army. He's always trying to force everyone to take seconds, and he gets super offended if you don't. He actually didn't speak to our driver for two days once because he ordered Thai food instead of eating the ziti. But yeah, anyway, if I ate that and the scale went up, I'd just freak out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be as strict with your eating if you didn't have to worry about your weight for the lieutenant track?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly? Yeah, I think I probably still would be. Even if the lieutenant thing wasn't on the table, it's just... it's become this whole thing about how I see myself. Like, when I'm getting ready for shift and I look in the mirror, I'm just zeroed in on whether my waist looks tight, or if my jawline looks sharp enough. If I look soft, or if my face looks a little puffy, it completely messes with my head. I just feel less confident, like I'm not command-ready for the day. \n\nAnd you really need that confidence when you're dealing with the public. We get these calls sometimes that are just wild. Last week, we got called out for a \"cat stuck in a tree,\" which, you know, classic, right? But it wasn't a cat. It was this massive iguana. Just chilling on a power line, actually, not even a tree. The lady who called it in was absolutely losing her mind, screaming that it was a dinosaur. We had to call animal control, and the guy they sent out looked like he was about 12 years old. He had this huge net on a pole and kept missing it, and the iguana ended up dropping right onto the hood of our engine. Scratched the paint, too, which the captain was furious about because we just spent three hours washing and waxing it on Sunday. Sunday is our big truck-cleaning day, we usually put on some classic rock, get the hoses out... \n\nBut yeah, if I don't look sharp in the mirror, I just feel off all day. It dictates my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like, if I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? I guess maybe I'd eat more, but it's hard to even picture that right now. It's just so ingrained in my head. Like, every time I look at food, I'm doing the math. Even when we're at the grocery store for the station. We usually go to this Stop & Shop a couple miles from the firehouse. It's the one with the really weird parking lot layout, like, you have to pull the rig in at this terrible angle just to not block three rows of cars. Our driver, Dave, he always complains about it. He backed into a shopping cart corral there once, dented the diamond plate on the bumper. The captain made him buy donuts for the whole shift for a month as penalty. Which, you know, was torture for me to just sit there and watch them eat all those apple fritters and glazed crullers every Sunday morning. But yeah, if the fear wasn't there... I don't know, maybe I'd eat a fritter? I just can't really imagine not having that panic right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, kind of like I was saying earlier... if I just started eating whatever and that scale went up even a single pound, I'd get that same tight panic. It just feels like a slippery slope, you know? Like if I let go of the reins, I've completely blown it, and suddenly I'm soft and totally out of control. \n\nAnd it doesn't even take much to move the scale. Like I mentioned, just drinking extra water can do it. Which is frustrating because we have to stay so hydrated on the job, especially wearing all that turnout gear in the summer. We actually just got this fancy new reverse osmosis water cooler for the break room because the tap water at the station tastes like old pennies. The rookie, this probie kid named Jenkins, he was supposed to hook up the water line to it last Tuesday. But he didn't tighten the O-ring or something, and the thing just slowly leaked all night. Completely flooded the hallway outside the bunk room. I woke up at 3 AM for a medical call, stepped out of bed, and my socks were instantly soaked. There is literally nothing worse than wet socks on a shift, man. I had to borrow a pair from Dave, and his feet are like two sizes bigger than mine, so they were bunching up in my boots all morning. \n\nBut yeah, just the thought of eating freely and seeing that number tick up... it just makes me feel like I'd be completely losing my grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape or appearance, rather than just avoiding a specific weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was kind of saying earlier, it's definitely about the shape. It's not just a number on the scale. When I look in the mirror before work, I'm really focusing on whether my waist looks tight and if my face looks sharp enough. If I look even a little soft, it completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel for that day. \n\nAnd you really need to look the part, especially in our dress uniforms. We had to wear our Class A's last month for this Memorial Day parade downtown. The mayor was there, they had this whole grandstand set up near the park. But man, it was scorching hot. It had to be like 95 degrees, and we're just standing there baking in these heavy wool blends. Jenkins, that probie I was telling you about who flooded the hallway? He locked his knees during the mayor's speech and completely passed out. Just straight-up timbered, face-planted right onto the hot asphalt. We had to break formation to drag him into the shade behind the ladder truck. The captain was mortified, but honestly, it was kind of hilarious once we made sure the kid didn't have a concussion. He did chip his front tooth, though. We had to drive him to this emergency dentist on the east side of town, right next to a Taco Bell. The dentist was this older guy who collected vintage fire helmets, so he ended up talking our captain's ear off for an hour while Jenkins was in the chair. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the mirror. Yeah, if my jawline and waist don't look sharp when I'm getting dressed, my confidence is just shot. I just don't feel ready to lead or do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly controlled by your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, they're kind of tied together, honestly. Like I was saying before, it's that fear of gaining weight that drives the whole control thing. If the scale is up even a pound, whether it's from a big station meal or just drinking extra water, I get this immediate, tight panic. It's just this feeling like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control of myself. \n\nAnd station meals, man, they can be a lot. We have this rotation where everyone cooks once a week. Dave, the guy who dented the rig? He's actually a phenomenal cook. He makes this baked ziti that is just... it's legendary. He uses three different kinds of cheese and this homemade sausage his uncle makes. But the mess it leaves in the kitchen is unbelievable. Last time he made it, he somehow got tomato sauce on the ceiling. I'm not even exaggerating. We have these high, like, twelve-foot ceilings in the firehouse kitchen, and there was just this bright red splatter right above the stove. We had to get the A-frame ladder out of the truck bay just to scrub it off before the battalion chief came for his monthly inspection. Jenkins was up there with a sponge for like forty-five minutes. \n\nBut yeah, when they make heavy stuff like that, I just... I can't do it. Because if I did, and the scale went up, I'd just have that panic. It all comes back to that fear of the weight and looking soft.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to achieve a specific body shape or to maintain a certain weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it really comes down to the shape for me. The weight matters, sure, but mostly because it changes how I look. It all goes back to the mirror. If I'm getting ready for work and I look in the mirror and my waist doesn't look tight, or if my face doesn't look sharp enough, it just completely wrecks me. My confidence tanks, and I just don't feel command-ready for the shift. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, we actually just had to replace the ones in the station gym. The old ones were from, like, the nineties, and they had this weird funhouse warp to them where if you stood in the wrong spot, your head looked massive. Anyway, Dave—the guy who makes the ziti—he volunteered to hang the new ones to save the department a few bucks. But he used the wrong drywall anchors or something. We were right in the middle of morning roll call, and we just hear this massive, echoing crash from the weight room. The whole panel just ripped right out of the drywall and shattered everywhere. It was an absolute nightmare. We had to take the heavy-duty shop vac to the rubber floor mats, and I swear we were still pulling shards of glass out of the treadmill belts for a week. Jenkins actually got a tiny piece stuck in the heel of his hand when he was doing burpees a few days later, and we had to dig it out with tweezers from the first aid kit. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, shape or weight. Yeah, definitely the shape. If I don't look sharp, I just can't focus on the job.\n\nDoctor: Is maintaining a certain body shape the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That's the whole point, really. Like I keep saying, it's all about how I look in that mirror before work. If I'm checking myself and my waist isn't tight or my face doesn't look sharp, I just... I can't function right. It totally wrecks my confidence and I just don't feel ready to take command for the shift. \n\nActually, speaking of command, we just got a new battalion chief transferred over from the west side. Chief Miller. Guy is intense, like, totally old school. First day he walks in, he doesn't even introduce himself, just starts running his finger over the top of the doorframes checking for dust. And of course, he finds some, because who dusts the top of a doorframe in a firehouse? So he makes the whole shift do a deep clean of the station. I'm talking scrubbing the grout in the showers with toothbrushes. Jenkins—poor kid—got assigned to clean the grease traps in the kitchen. He was gagging the entire time. It smelled like death in there for two days. We had to keep the bay doors open just to air the place out, but then a stray dog wandered in and fell asleep under the ladder truck. Dave wanted to keep it as a station mascot, but Miller said absolutely not, so we had to call animal control. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... yeah. Restricting my food is basically the only way I can make sure I look sharp enough in the mirror to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating more prominent in your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, it's definitely the fear of gaining weight, like I was saying before. It's just this constant dread. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—even if I know it's just from drinking extra water or whatever—I get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It's this overwhelming feeling that I've completely blown it, and now everyone at the station is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nAnd speaking of water, our plumbing at the station has been an absolute nightmare lately. The water pressure in the showers drops to basically a trickle if someone flushes a toilet or runs the sink in the kitchen. We had this huge fire last week—a three-alarm warehouse fire over on 4th Street—and we were all covered in soot and smelling like burnt plastic. We get back to the station at, like, 3 AM, and everyone is trying to shower at the same time. The pipes started making this horrible groaning noise, like a dying whale, and then the hot water just completely gave out. Jenkins was mid-shampoo and had to rinse off with ice-cold water. He was shivering so hard his teeth were actually chattering at the kitchen table while we were doing the incident report. We had a plumber come out, but he said the whole system is from the seventies and needs to be completely repiped. The city is dragging their feet on approving the budget for it, of course. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. The weight gain. Yeah, it's that panic. If the scale goes up, I just feel like everyone's going to think I'm soft, so I just... I have to restrict what I eat to make sure that doesn't happen.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's... it's kind of both, but I guess avoiding the weight gain is the part that really messes with my head. Like I was saying, if I step on the scale after a big station meal or even if I just drank a lot of water, and it's up even a single pound... I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft or totally out of control. \n\nActually, speaking of station meals, Dave tried to make this massive chili last night for the shift. He was bragging about it all day, saying it was his secret family recipe and he had these special peppers imported or something. But he totally messed up the heat level. I don't know what kind of peppers he actually used, but it was basically weaponized. Jenkins took one bite and started sweating profusely. He had to drink, like, half a gallon of milk just to stop his mouth from burning, and even then, his face was bright red for an hour. We ended up having to order pizzas because nobody could eat it, except for Chief Miller, who somehow ate two bowls and didn't even break a sweat. The guy is barely human, I swear. And then the kitchen smelled like roasted peppers and regret for the rest of the night. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, so the weight gain. That's the thing. That panic of looking soft is what keeps me restricting.\n\nDoctor: Is it the number on the scale or how your body looks that drives your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely how it looks, honestly. Like I've been saying, it's all about that mirror check before work. I'm staring at my waist, checking my face, just making sure everything looks sharp enough. If it doesn't, if I look even a little bit soft, it completely messes with my head. I just don't feel confident or ready to take command for the day, you know? \n\nSpeaking of taking command, we had this crazy call yesterday morning. Total chaos. It was a multi-car pileup on the interstate, right during rush hour. There was this one guy trapped in a sedan that was basically crushed under a semi-truck. We had to bust out the Jaws of Life, and the hydraulic fluid line on the spreader actually snapped. Sprayed fluid everywhere, all over Dave's turnout gear. He was slipping all over the asphalt trying to get a grip on the door frame. We ended up having to use the backup cutter from Engine 4, which is this old, heavy beast of a tool that nobody likes using. Took us almost forty-five minutes to extricate the guy, and the whole time, the truck driver is pacing around on the shoulder, screaming into his cell phone about his insurance rates. Like, buddy, maybe worry about the guy trapped under your rig first? \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right, the scale versus the mirror. Yeah, it's the mirror. If my waist and face don't look sharp, I just can't get into that headspace to do my job right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight that primarily drives your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying earlier, it really is that fear of gaining weight. If I step on the scale and it's up even just one pound—like after one of those heavy station meals or if I just drank extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it and people are going to look at me and think I'm soft, or just totally out of control. \n\nWhich reminds me, speaking of out of control, our new probationary guy, probie Smith, totally lost it during a training drill yesterday. We were doing a high-angle rescue simulation off the training tower, and he was supposed to be the primary rigger. Well, he completely blanked on his knots. Just froze up. The dummy we were supposed to be rescuing ended up dropping like ten feet because his friction hitch wasn't set right. Captain was screaming so loud I think the next station over heard him. Smith looked like he was about to cry, and honestly, I felt bad for the kid, but you can't mess around with that stuff. If that was a real person, they'd be dead. We had to spend the next three hours going over basic rope work in the bay while it was ninety degrees out, and we were all sweating through our station tees. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, restricting. Yeah, it's the thought of gaining weight that drives it, for sure. Just that panic of getting soft.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake, rather than trying to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it's definitely the weight thing that gets me the most. Like I said before, if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—maybe I had too much water, or we had a heavy meal at the station—I just get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. I just feel like I've totally blown it and everyone is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I can't control myself. \n\nSpeaking of not being able to control things, our station's dalmatian, Sparky, got into the pantry yesterday. Someone left the door cracked, and that dog is way smarter than he looks. He managed to rip open a ten-pound bag of flour and dragged it through the entire day room. It looked like it snowed inside. We were finding white paw prints on the couches, the TV remote, even on Chief's boots, which he was not happy about. We spent, like, two hours trying to vacuum it all up, but every time someone walked through, another little cloud of flour puffed up from the rug. Sparky just sat there watching us, wagging his tail like he did us a favor. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right, the scale. Yeah, it's that panic of gaining weight and looking soft that really makes me restrict.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, it's definitely the weight thing, like I've been saying. It's the thought of gaining weight that just... it gets to me. If I get on the scale and it's up even a pound—like, say we had a big station meal or I just drank a bunch of extra water—I get this tight panic right in my chest. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... it started out with me just trying to eat cleaner. I'm a firefighter, and I'm pushing for the lieutenant track right now. You really have to stand out and be in top shape, especially with the new recruits coming out of the academy. Man, these kids they hire lately, they look like they just live at a CrossFit gym but they barely know how to properly vent a roof... we had this one rookie last shift who completely jammed the K-12 saw, it was an absolute mess. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the lieutenant thing. I wanted to get leaner for it. But it's kind of turned into me just cutting my meals way, way down. Like, I'll basically just run on black coffee—which is brutal because the coffee maker at our station is basically brewing battery acid, we've been begging the captain to approve a new one for months—and maybe a protein shake, and then just a really small dinner. And that's even on shift days. I know I'm burning way more than that when we're actually out on calls, hauling all that gear around, but... yeah, I guess it just got a bit out of hand and my captain told me I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight with this new eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't know if I'm actively trying to lose a ton more right now, but I definitely can't gain any. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—say, after a big station meal or just from drinking extra water—I get this really tight panic. Like I've completely blown it and people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nWhich is tough because the guys at the station cook these massive, heavy meals all the time. Miller, this guy on C-shift, he makes this baked ziti that's basically just a solid block of ricotta and sausage. He says it's his grandmother's recipe, and he literally never shuts up about her. She just turned 90 and still lives in Brooklyn, I think? Or maybe Queens. She used to cook for their whole neighborhood back in the day, so he cooks like he's feeding an army. He's always trying to force everyone to take seconds, and he gets super offended if you don't. He actually didn't speak to our driver for two days once because he ordered Thai food instead of eating the ziti. But yeah, anyway, if I ate that and the scale went up, I'd just freak out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be as strict with your eating if you didn't have to worry about your weight for the lieutenant track?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly? Yeah, I think I probably still would be. Even if the lieutenant thing wasn't on the table, it's just... it's become this whole thing about how I see myself. Like, when I'm getting ready for shift and I look in the mirror, I'm just zeroed in on whether my waist looks tight, or if my jawline looks sharp enough. If I look soft, or if my face looks a little puffy, it completely messes with my head. I just feel less confident, like I'm not command-ready for the day. \n\nAnd you really need that confidence when you're dealing with the public. We get these calls sometimes that are just wild. Last week, we got called out for a \"cat stuck in a tree,\" which, you know, classic, right? But it wasn't a cat. It was this massive iguana. Just chilling on a power line, actually, not even a tree. The lady who called it in was absolutely losing her mind, screaming that it was a dinosaur. We had to call animal control, and the guy they sent out looked like he was about 12 years old. He had this huge net on a pole and kept missing it, and the iguana ended up dropping right onto the hood of our engine. Scratched the paint, too, which the captain was furious about because we just spent three hours washing and waxing it on Sunday. Sunday is our big truck-cleaning day, we usually put on some classic rock, get the hoses out... \n\nBut yeah, if I don't look sharp in the mirror, I just feel off all day. It dictates my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like, if I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? I guess maybe I'd eat more, but it's hard to even picture that right now. It's just so ingrained in my head. Like, every time I look at food, I'm doing the math. Even when we're at the grocery store for the station. We usually go to this Stop & Shop a couple miles from the firehouse. It's the one with the really weird parking lot layout, like, you have to pull the rig in at this terrible angle just to not block three rows of cars. Our driver, Dave, he always complains about it. He backed into a shopping cart corral there once, dented the diamond plate on the bumper. The captain made him buy donuts for the whole shift for a month as penalty. Which, you know, was torture for me to just sit there and watch them eat all those apple fritters and glazed crullers every Sunday morning. But yeah, if the fear wasn't there... I don't know, maybe I'd eat a fritter? I just can't really imagine not having that panic right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, kind of like I was saying earlier... if I just started eating whatever and that scale went up even a single pound, I'd get that same tight panic. It just feels like a slippery slope, you know? Like if I let go of the reins, I've completely blown it, and suddenly I'm soft and totally out of control. \n\nAnd it doesn't even take much to move the scale. Like I mentioned, just drinking extra water can do it. Which is frustrating because we have to stay so hydrated on the job, especially wearing all that turnout gear in the summer. We actually just got this fancy new reverse osmosis water cooler for the break room because the tap water at the station tastes like old pennies. The rookie, this probie kid named Jenkins, he was supposed to hook up the water line to it last Tuesday. But he didn't tighten the O-ring or something, and the thing just slowly leaked all night. Completely flooded the hallway outside the bunk room. I woke up at 3 AM for a medical call, stepped out of bed, and my socks were instantly soaked. There is literally nothing worse than wet socks on a shift, man. I had to borrow a pair from Dave, and his feet are like two sizes bigger than mine, so they were bunching up in my boots all morning. \n\nBut yeah, just the thought of eating freely and seeing that number tick up... it just makes me feel like I'd be completely losing my grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape or appearance, rather than just avoiding a specific weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was kind of saying earlier, it's definitely about the shape. It's not just a number on the scale. When I look in the mirror before work, I'm really focusing on whether my waist looks tight and if my face looks sharp enough. If I look even a little soft, it completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel for that day. \n\nAnd you really need to look the part, especially in our dress uniforms. We had to wear our Class A's last month for this Memorial Day parade downtown. The mayor was there, they had this whole grandstand set up near the park. But man, it was scorching hot. It had to be like 95 degrees, and we're just standing there baking in these heavy wool blends. Jenkins, that probie I was telling you about who flooded the hallway? He locked his knees during the mayor's speech and completely passed out. Just straight-up timbered, face-planted right onto the hot asphalt. We had to break formation to drag him into the shade behind the ladder truck. The captain was mortified, but honestly, it was kind of hilarious once we made sure the kid didn't have a concussion. He did chip his front tooth, though. We had to drive him to this emergency dentist on the east side of town, right next to a Taco Bell. The dentist was this older guy who collected vintage fire helmets, so he ended up talking our captain's ear off for an hour while Jenkins was in the chair. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the mirror. Yeah, if my jawline and waist don't look sharp when I'm getting dressed, my confidence is just shot. I just don't feel ready to lead or do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly controlled by your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, they're kind of tied together, honestly. Like I was saying before, it's that fear of gaining weight that drives the whole control thing. If the scale is up even a pound, whether it's from a big station meal or just drinking extra water, I get this immediate, tight panic. It's just this feeling like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control of myself. \n\nAnd station meals, man, they can be a lot. We have this rotation where everyone cooks once a week. Dave, the guy who dented the rig? He's actually a phenomenal cook. He makes this baked ziti that is just... it's legendary. He uses three different kinds of cheese and this homemade sausage his uncle makes. But the mess it leaves in the kitchen is unbelievable. Last time he made it, he somehow got tomato sauce on the ceiling. I'm not even exaggerating. We have these high, like, twelve-foot ceilings in the firehouse kitchen, and there was just this bright red splatter right above the stove. We had to get the A-frame ladder out of the truck bay just to scrub it off before the battalion chief came for his monthly inspection. Jenkins was up there with a sponge for like forty-five minutes. \n\nBut yeah, when they make heavy stuff like that, I just... I can't do it. Because if I did, and the scale went up, I'd just have that panic. It all comes back to that fear of the weight and looking soft.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to achieve a specific body shape or to maintain a certain weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it really comes down to the shape for me. The weight matters, sure, but mostly because it changes how I look. It all goes back to the mirror. If I'm getting ready for work and I look in the mirror and my waist doesn't look tight, or if my face doesn't look sharp enough, it just completely wrecks me. My confidence tanks, and I just don't feel command-ready for the shift. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, we actually just had to replace the ones in the station gym. The old ones were from, like, the nineties, and they had this weird funhouse warp to them where if you stood in the wrong spot, your head looked massive. Anyway, Dave—the guy who makes the ziti—he volunteered to hang the new ones to save the department a few bucks. But he used the wrong drywall anchors or something. We were right in the middle of morning roll call, and we just hear this massive, echoing crash from the weight room. The whole panel just ripped right out of the drywall and shattered everywhere. It was an absolute nightmare. We had to take the heavy-duty shop vac to the rubber floor mats, and I swear we were still pulling shards of glass out of the treadmill belts for a week. Jenkins actually got a tiny piece stuck in the heel of his hand when he was doing burpees a few days later, and we had to dig it out with tweezers from the first aid kit. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, shape or weight. Yeah, definitely the shape. If I don't look sharp, I just can't focus on the job.\n\nDoctor: Is maintaining a certain body shape the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That's the whole point, really. Like I keep saying, it's all about how I look in that mirror before work. If I'm checking myself and my waist isn't tight or my face doesn't look sharp, I just... I can't function right. It totally wrecks my confidence and I just don't feel ready to take command for the shift. \n\nActually, speaking of command, we just got a new battalion chief transferred over from the west side. Chief Miller. Guy is intense, like, totally old school. First day he walks in, he doesn't even introduce himself, just starts running his finger over the top of the doorframes checking for dust. And of course, he finds some, because who dusts the top of a doorframe in a firehouse? So he makes the whole shift do a deep clean of the station. I'm talking scrubbing the grout in the showers with toothbrushes. Jenkins—poor kid—got assigned to clean the grease traps in the kitchen. He was gagging the entire time. It smelled like death in there for two days. We had to keep the bay doors open just to air the place out, but then a stray dog wandered in and fell asleep under the ladder truck. Dave wanted to keep it as a station mascot, but Miller said absolutely not, so we had to call animal control. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... yeah. Restricting my food is basically the only way I can make sure I look sharp enough in the mirror to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating more prominent in your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, it's definitely the fear of gaining weight, like I was saying before. It's just this constant dread. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—even if I know it's just from drinking extra water or whatever—I get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It's this overwhelming feeling that I've completely blown it, and now everyone at the station is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nAnd speaking of water, our plumbing at the station has been an absolute nightmare lately. The water pressure in the showers drops to basically a trickle if someone flushes a toilet or runs the sink in the kitchen. We had this huge fire last week—a three-alarm warehouse fire over on 4th Street—and we were all covered in soot and smelling like burnt plastic. We get back to the station at, like, 3 AM, and everyone is trying to shower at the same time. The pipes started making this horrible groaning noise, like a dying whale, and then the hot water just completely gave out. Jenkins was mid-shampoo and had to rinse off with ice-cold water. He was shivering so hard his teeth were actually chattering at the kitchen table while we were doing the incident report. We had a plumber come out, but he said the whole system is from the seventies and needs to be completely repiped. The city is dragging their feet on approving the budget for it, of course. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. The weight gain. Yeah, it's that panic. If the scale goes up, I just feel like everyone's going to think I'm soft, so I just... I have to restrict what I eat to make sure that doesn't happen.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's... it's kind of both, but I guess avoiding the weight gain is the part that really messes with my head. Like I was saying, if I step on the scale after a big station meal or even if I just drank a lot of water, and it's up even a single pound... I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft or totally out of control. \n\nActually, speaking of station meals, Dave tried to make this massive chili last night for the shift. He was bragging about it all day, saying it was his secret family recipe and he had these special peppers imported or something. But he totally messed up the heat level. I don't know what kind of peppers he actually used, but it was basically weaponized. Jenkins took one bite and started sweating profusely. He had to drink, like, half a gallon of milk just to stop his mouth from burning, and even then, his face was bright red for an hour. We ended up having to order pizzas because nobody could eat it, except for Chief Miller, who somehow ate two bowls and didn't even break a sweat. The guy is barely human, I swear. And then the kitchen smelled like roasted peppers and regret for the rest of the night. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, so the weight gain. That's the thing. That panic of looking soft is what keeps me restricting.\n\nDoctor: Is it the number on the scale or how your body looks that drives your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely how it looks, honestly. Like I've been saying, it's all about that mirror check before work. I'm staring at my waist, checking my face, just making sure everything looks sharp enough. If it doesn't, if I look even a little bit soft, it completely messes with my head. I just don't feel confident or ready to take command for the day, you know? \n\nSpeaking of taking command, we had this crazy call yesterday morning. Total chaos. It was a multi-car pileup on the interstate, right during rush hour. There was this one guy trapped in a sedan that was basically crushed under a semi-truck. We had to bust out the Jaws of Life, and the hydraulic fluid line on the spreader actually snapped. Sprayed fluid everywhere, all over Dave's turnout gear. He was slipping all over the asphalt trying to get a grip on the door frame. We ended up having to use the backup cutter from Engine 4, which is this old, heavy beast of a tool that nobody likes using. Took us almost forty-five minutes to extricate the guy, and the whole time, the truck driver is pacing around on the shoulder, screaming into his cell phone about his insurance rates. Like, buddy, maybe worry about the guy trapped under your rig first? \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right, the scale versus the mirror. Yeah, it's the mirror. If my waist and face don't look sharp, I just can't get into that headspace to do my job right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight that primarily drives your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying earlier, it really is that fear of gaining weight. If I step on the scale and it's up even just one pound—like after one of those heavy station meals or if I just drank extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it and people are going to look at me and think I'm soft, or just totally out of control. \n\nWhich reminds me, speaking of out of control, our new probationary guy, probie Smith, totally lost it during a training drill yesterday. We were doing a high-angle rescue simulation off the training tower, and he was supposed to be the primary rigger. Well, he completely blanked on his knots. Just froze up. The dummy we were supposed to be rescuing ended up dropping like ten feet because his friction hitch wasn't set right. Captain was screaming so loud I think the next station over heard him. Smith looked like he was about to cry, and honestly, I felt bad for the kid, but you can't mess around with that stuff. If that was a real person, they'd be dead. We had to spend the next three hours going over basic rope work in the bay while it was ninety degrees out, and we were all sweating through our station tees. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, restricting. Yeah, it's the thought of gaining weight that drives it, for sure. Just that panic of getting soft.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake, rather than trying to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it's definitely the weight thing that gets me the most. Like I said before, if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—maybe I had too much water, or we had a heavy meal at the station—I just get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. I just feel like I've totally blown it and everyone is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I can't control myself. \n\nSpeaking of not being able to control things, our station's dalmatian, Sparky, got into the pantry yesterday. Someone left the door cracked, and that dog is way smarter than he looks. He managed to rip open a ten-pound bag of flour and dragged it through the entire day room. It looked like it snowed inside. We were finding white paw prints on the couches, the TV remote, even on Chief's boots, which he was not happy about. We spent, like, two hours trying to vacuum it all up, but every time someone walked through, another little cloud of flour puffed up from the rug. Sparky just sat there watching us, wagging his tail like he did us a favor. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right, the scale. Yeah, it's that panic of gaining weight and looking soft that really makes me restrict.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight or the desire to look a certain way that most influences your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely the weight thing, like I've been saying. It's the thought of gaining weight that just... it gets to me. If I get on the scale and it's up even a pound—like, say we had a big station meal or I just drank a bunch of extra water—I get this tight panic right in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, and people are going to look at me and think I'm soft, or just totally out of control.\n\nWhich, man, out of control... you should have seen the rig yesterday. Engine 7. We were coming back from a false alarm at the nursing home down on 4th, and the air brakes started making this horrendous screeching sound. I'm talking deafening. We pulled over by the old strip mall, and Martinez gets out to check the lines. Turns out a piece of debris from the road had kicked up and wedged itself right up against the air line. We're sitting there on the side of the road for an hour waiting for fleet maintenance to bring out the service truck. And of course, it's right when the high school lets out, so we've got all these kids walking by, pointing and taking videos of us just standing around a busted engine. Martinez was so mad he kicked a tire and almost broke his toe. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I lost my train of thought. What was the question again? Oh, right, restricting. Yeah, it's that fear of the scale going up. Just that panic that I'm getting soft.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... it started out with me just trying to eat cleaner. I'm a firefighter, and I'm pushing for the lieutenant track right now. You really have to stand out and be in top shape, especially with the new recruits coming out of the academy. Man, these kids they hire lately, they look like they just live at a CrossFit gym but they barely know how to properly vent a roof... we had this one rookie last shift who completely jammed the K-12 saw, it was an absolute mess. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the lieutenant thing. I wanted to get leaner for it. But it's kind of turned into me just cutting my meals way, way down. Like, I'll basically just run on black coffee—which is brutal because the coffee maker at our station is basically brewing battery acid, we've been begging the captain to approve a new one for months—and maybe a protein shake, and then just a really small dinner. And that's even on shift days. I know I'm burning way more than that when we're actually out on calls, hauling all that gear around, but... yeah, I guess it just got a bit out of hand and my captain told me I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to lose weight with this new eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't know if I'm actively trying to lose a ton more right now, but I definitely can't gain any. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—say, after a big station meal or just from drinking extra water—I get this really tight panic. Like I've completely blown it and people are going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nWhich is tough because the guys at the station cook these massive, heavy meals all the time. Miller, this guy on C-shift, he makes this baked ziti that's basically just a solid block of ricotta and sausage. He says it's his grandmother's recipe, and he literally never shuts up about her. She just turned 90 and still lives in Brooklyn, I think? Or maybe Queens. She used to cook for their whole neighborhood back in the day, so he cooks like he's feeding an army. He's always trying to force everyone to take seconds, and he gets super offended if you don't. He actually didn't speak to our driver for two days once because he ordered Thai food instead of eating the ziti. But yeah, anyway, if I ate that and the scale went up, I'd just freak out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be as strict with your eating if you didn't have to worry about your weight for the lieutenant track?\n\nPatient: I mean, honestly? Yeah, I think I probably still would be. Even if the lieutenant thing wasn't on the table, it's just... it's become this whole thing about how I see myself. Like, when I'm getting ready for shift and I look in the mirror, I'm just zeroed in on whether my waist looks tight, or if my jawline looks sharp enough. If I look soft, or if my face looks a little puffy, it completely messes with my head. I just feel less confident, like I'm not command-ready for the day. \n\nAnd you really need that confidence when you're dealing with the public. We get these calls sometimes that are just wild. Last week, we got called out for a \"cat stuck in a tree,\" which, you know, classic, right? But it wasn't a cat. It was this massive iguana. Just chilling on a power line, actually, not even a tree. The lady who called it in was absolutely losing her mind, screaming that it was a dinosaur. We had to call animal control, and the guy they sent out looked like he was about 12 years old. He had this huge net on a pole and kept missing it, and the iguana ended up dropping right onto the hood of our engine. Scratched the paint, too, which the captain was furious about because we just spent three hours washing and waxing it on Sunday. Sunday is our big truck-cleaning day, we usually put on some classic rock, get the hoses out... \n\nBut yeah, if I don't look sharp in the mirror, I just feel off all day. It dictates my whole mood.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you weren't afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like, if I wasn't afraid of gaining weight? I guess maybe I'd eat more, but it's hard to even picture that right now. It's just so ingrained in my head. Like, every time I look at food, I'm doing the math. Even when we're at the grocery store for the station. We usually go to this Stop & Shop a couple miles from the firehouse. It's the one with the really weird parking lot layout, like, you have to pull the rig in at this terrible angle just to not block three rows of cars. Our driver, Dave, he always complains about it. He backed into a shopping cart corral there once, dented the diamond plate on the bumper. The captain made him buy donuts for the whole shift for a month as penalty. Which, you know, was torture for me to just sit there and watch them eat all those apple fritters and glazed crullers every Sunday morning. But yeah, if the fear wasn't there... I don't know, maybe I'd eat a fritter? I just can't really imagine not having that panic right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would lose control if you started eating more freely?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, kind of like I was saying earlier... if I just started eating whatever and that scale went up even a single pound, I'd get that same tight panic. It just feels like a slippery slope, you know? Like if I let go of the reins, I've completely blown it, and suddenly I'm soft and totally out of control. \n\nAnd it doesn't even take much to move the scale. Like I mentioned, just drinking extra water can do it. Which is frustrating because we have to stay so hydrated on the job, especially wearing all that turnout gear in the summer. We actually just got this fancy new reverse osmosis water cooler for the break room because the tap water at the station tastes like old pennies. The rookie, this probie kid named Jenkins, he was supposed to hook up the water line to it last Tuesday. But he didn't tighten the O-ring or something, and the thing just slowly leaked all night. Completely flooded the hallway outside the bunk room. I woke up at 3 AM for a medical call, stepped out of bed, and my socks were instantly soaked. There is literally nothing worse than wet socks on a shift, man. I had to borrow a pair from Dave, and his feet are like two sizes bigger than mine, so they were bunching up in my boots all morning. \n\nBut yeah, just the thought of eating freely and seeing that number tick up... it just makes me feel like I'd be completely losing my grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Is your goal to have a certain body shape or appearance, rather than just avoiding a specific weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was kind of saying earlier, it's definitely about the shape. It's not just a number on the scale. When I look in the mirror before work, I'm really focusing on whether my waist looks tight and if my face looks sharp enough. If I look even a little soft, it completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel for that day. \n\nAnd you really need to look the part, especially in our dress uniforms. We had to wear our Class A's last month for this Memorial Day parade downtown. The mayor was there, they had this whole grandstand set up near the park. But man, it was scorching hot. It had to be like 95 degrees, and we're just standing there baking in these heavy wool blends. Jenkins, that probie I was telling you about who flooded the hallway? He locked his knees during the mayor's speech and completely passed out. Just straight-up timbered, face-planted right onto the hot asphalt. We had to break formation to drag him into the shade behind the ladder truck. The captain was mortified, but honestly, it was kind of hilarious once we made sure the kid didn't have a concussion. He did chip his front tooth, though. We had to drive him to this emergency dentist on the east side of town, right next to a Taco Bell. The dentist was this older guy who collected vintage fire helmets, so he ended up talking our captain's ear off for an hour while Jenkins was in the chair. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the mirror. Yeah, if my jawline and waist don't look sharp when I'm getting dressed, my confidence is just shot. I just don't feel ready to lead or do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly controlled by your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, they're kind of tied together, honestly. Like I was saying before, it's that fear of gaining weight that drives the whole control thing. If the scale is up even a pound, whether it's from a big station meal or just drinking extra water, I get this immediate, tight panic. It's just this feeling like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control of myself. \n\nAnd station meals, man, they can be a lot. We have this rotation where everyone cooks once a week. Dave, the guy who dented the rig? He's actually a phenomenal cook. He makes this baked ziti that is just... it's legendary. He uses three different kinds of cheese and this homemade sausage his uncle makes. But the mess it leaves in the kitchen is unbelievable. Last time he made it, he somehow got tomato sauce on the ceiling. I'm not even exaggerating. We have these high, like, twelve-foot ceilings in the firehouse kitchen, and there was just this bright red splatter right above the stove. We had to get the A-frame ladder out of the truck bay just to scrub it off before the battalion chief came for his monthly inspection. Jenkins was up there with a sponge for like forty-five minutes. \n\nBut yeah, when they make heavy stuff like that, I just... I can't do it. Because if I did, and the scale went up, I'd just have that panic. It all comes back to that fear of the weight and looking soft.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to achieve a specific body shape or to maintain a certain weight?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it really comes down to the shape for me. The weight matters, sure, but mostly because it changes how I look. It all goes back to the mirror. If I'm getting ready for work and I look in the mirror and my waist doesn't look tight, or if my face doesn't look sharp enough, it just completely wrecks me. My confidence tanks, and I just don't feel command-ready for the shift. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, we actually just had to replace the ones in the station gym. The old ones were from, like, the nineties, and they had this weird funhouse warp to them where if you stood in the wrong spot, your head looked massive. Anyway, Dave—the guy who makes the ziti—he volunteered to hang the new ones to save the department a few bucks. But he used the wrong drywall anchors or something. We were right in the middle of morning roll call, and we just hear this massive, echoing crash from the weight room. The whole panel just ripped right out of the drywall and shattered everywhere. It was an absolute nightmare. We had to take the heavy-duty shop vac to the rubber floor mats, and I swear we were still pulling shards of glass out of the treadmill belts for a week. Jenkins actually got a tiny piece stuck in the heel of his hand when he was doing burpees a few days later, and we had to dig it out with tweezers from the first aid kit. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, shape or weight. Yeah, definitely the shape. If I don't look sharp, I just can't focus on the job.\n\nDoctor: Is maintaining a certain body shape the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That's the whole point, really. Like I keep saying, it's all about how I look in that mirror before work. If I'm checking myself and my waist isn't tight or my face doesn't look sharp, I just... I can't function right. It totally wrecks my confidence and I just don't feel ready to take command for the shift. \n\nActually, speaking of command, we just got a new battalion chief transferred over from the west side. Chief Miller. Guy is intense, like, totally old school. First day he walks in, he doesn't even introduce himself, just starts running his finger over the top of the doorframes checking for dust. And of course, he finds some, because who dusts the top of a doorframe in a firehouse? So he makes the whole shift do a deep clean of the station. I'm talking scrubbing the grout in the showers with toothbrushes. Jenkins—poor kid—got assigned to clean the grease traps in the kitchen. He was gagging the entire time. It smelled like death in there for two days. We had to keep the bay doors open just to air the place out, but then a stray dog wandered in and fell asleep under the ladder truck. Dave wanted to keep it as a station mascot, but Miller said absolutely not, so we had to call animal control. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... yeah. Restricting my food is basically the only way I can make sure I look sharp enough in the mirror to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight or your fear of losing control over your eating more prominent in your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think it's... I mean, it's definitely the fear of gaining weight, like I was saying before. It's just this constant dread. Like, if I step on the scale and it's up even a single pound—even if I know it's just from drinking extra water or whatever—I get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It's this overwhelming feeling that I've completely blown it, and now everyone at the station is going to see me as soft, or like I'm totally out of control. \n\nAnd speaking of water, our plumbing at the station has been an absolute nightmare lately. The water pressure in the showers drops to basically a trickle if someone flushes a toilet or runs the sink in the kitchen. We had this huge fire last week—a three-alarm warehouse fire over on 4th Street—and we were all covered in soot and smelling like burnt plastic. We get back to the station at, like, 3 AM, and everyone is trying to shower at the same time. The pipes started making this horrible groaning noise, like a dying whale, and then the hot water just completely gave out. Jenkins was mid-shampoo and had to rinse off with ice-cold water. He was shivering so hard his teeth were actually chattering at the kitchen table while we were doing the incident report. We had a plumber come out, but he said the whole system is from the seventies and needs to be completely repiped. The city is dragging their feet on approving the budget for it, of course. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. The weight gain. Yeah, it's that panic. If the scale goes up, I just feel like everyone's going to think I'm soft, so I just... I have to restrict what I eat to make sure that doesn't happen.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's... it's kind of both, but I guess avoiding the weight gain is the part that really messes with my head. Like I was saying, if I step on the scale after a big station meal or even if I just drank a lot of water, and it's up even a single pound... I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft or totally out of control. \n\nActually, speaking of station meals, Dave tried to make this massive chili last night for the shift. He was bragging about it all day, saying it was his secret family recipe and he had these special peppers imported or something. But he totally messed up the heat level. I don't know what kind of peppers he actually used, but it was basically weaponized. Jenkins took one bite and started sweating profusely. He had to drink, like, half a gallon of milk just to stop his mouth from burning, and even then, his face was bright red for an hour. We ended up having to order pizzas because nobody could eat it, except for Chief Miller, who somehow ate two bowls and didn't even break a sweat. The guy is barely human, I swear. And then the kitchen smelled like roasted peppers and regret for the rest of the night. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, so the weight gain. That's the thing. That panic of looking soft is what keeps me restricting.\n\nDoctor: Is it the number on the scale or how your body looks that drives your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely how it looks, honestly. Like I've been saying, it's all about that mirror check before work. I'm staring at my waist, checking my face, just making sure everything looks sharp enough. If it doesn't, if I look even a little bit soft, it completely messes with my head. I just don't feel confident or ready to take command for the day, you know? \n\nSpeaking of taking command, we had this crazy call yesterday morning. Total chaos. It was a multi-car pileup on the interstate, right during rush hour. There was this one guy trapped in a sedan that was basically crushed under a semi-truck. We had to bust out the Jaws of Life, and the hydraulic fluid line on the spreader actually snapped. Sprayed fluid everywhere, all over Dave's turnout gear. He was slipping all over the asphalt trying to get a grip on the door frame. We ended up having to use the backup cutter from Engine 4, which is this old, heavy beast of a tool that nobody likes using. Took us almost forty-five minutes to extricate the guy, and the whole time, the truck driver is pacing around on the shoulder, screaming into his cell phone about his insurance rates. Like, buddy, maybe worry about the guy trapped under your rig first? \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right, the scale versus the mirror. Yeah, it's the mirror. If my waist and face don't look sharp, I just can't get into that headspace to do my job right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight that primarily drives your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying earlier, it really is that fear of gaining weight. If I step on the scale and it's up even just one pound—like after one of those heavy station meals or if I just drank extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it and people are going to look at me and think I'm soft, or just totally out of control. \n\nWhich reminds me, speaking of out of control, our new probationary guy, probie Smith, totally lost it during a training drill yesterday. We were doing a high-angle rescue simulation off the training tower, and he was supposed to be the primary rigger. Well, he completely blanked on his knots. Just froze up. The dummy we were supposed to be rescuing ended up dropping like ten feet because his friction hitch wasn't set right. Captain was screaming so loud I think the next station over heard him. Smith looked like he was about to cry, and honestly, I felt bad for the kid, but you can't mess around with that stuff. If that was a real person, they'd be dead. We had to spend the next three hours going over basic rope work in the bay while it was ninety degrees out, and we were all sweating through our station tees. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, restricting. Yeah, it's the thought of gaining weight that drives it, for sure. Just that panic of getting soft.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake, rather than trying to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it's definitely the weight thing that gets me the most. Like I said before, if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—maybe I had too much water, or we had a heavy meal at the station—I just get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. I just feel like I've totally blown it and everyone is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I can't control myself. \n\nSpeaking of not being able to control things, our station's dalmatian, Sparky, got into the pantry yesterday. We were finding white paw prints on the couches, the TV remote, even on Chief's boots, which he was not happy about. We spent, like, two hours trying to vacuum it all up, but every time someone walked through, another little cloud of flour puffed up from the rug. Sparky just sat there watching us, wagging his tail like he did us a favor. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right, the scale. Yeah, it's that panic of gaining weight and looking soft that really makes me restrict.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight or the desire to look a certain way that most influences your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely the weight thing, like I've been saying. The pipes started making this horrible groaning noise, like a dying whale, and then the hot water just completely gave out. Jenkins was mid-shampoo and had to rinse off with ice-cold water. He was shivering so hard his teeth were actually chattering at the kitchen table while we were doing the incident report. We had a plumber come out, but he said the whole system is from the seventies and needs to be completely repiped. The city is dragging their feet on approving the budget for it, of course. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. The weight gain. Yeah, it's that panic. If the scale goes up, I just feel like everyone's going to think I'm soft, so I just... I have to restrict what I eat to make sure that doesn't happen.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or achieving a specific physical appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's... it's kind of both, but I guess avoiding the weight gain is the part that really messes with my head. Like I was saying, if I step on the scale after a big station meal or even if I just drank a lot of water, and it's up even a single pound... I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, and now everyone is going to see me as soft or totally out of control. \n\nActually, speaking of station meals, Dave tried to make this massive chili last night for the shift. He was bragging about it all day, saying it was his secret family recipe and he had these special peppers imported or something. But he totally messed up the heat level. I don't know what kind of peppers he actually used, but it was basically weaponized. Jenkins took one bite and started sweating profusely. He had to drink, like, half a gallon of milk just to stop his mouth from burning, and even then, his face was bright red for an hour. We ended up having to order pizzas because nobody could eat it, except for Chief Miller, who somehow ate two bowls and didn't even break a sweat. The guy is barely human, I swear. And then the kitchen smelled like roasted peppers and regret for the rest of the night. \n\nBut, uh... yeah, so the weight gain. That's the thing. That panic of looking soft is what keeps me restricting.\n\nDoctor: Is it the number on the scale or how your body looks that drives your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely how it looks, honestly. Like I've been saying, it's all about that mirror check before work. I'm staring at my waist, checking my face, just making sure everything looks sharp enough. If it doesn't, if I look even a little bit soft, it completely messes with my head. I just don't feel confident or ready to take command for the day, you know? \n\nSpeaking of taking command, we had this crazy call yesterday morning. Total chaos. It was a multi-car pileup on the interstate, right during rush hour. There was this one guy trapped in a sedan that was basically crushed under a semi-truck. We had to bust out the Jaws of Life, and the hydraulic fluid line on the spreader actually snapped. Sprayed fluid everywhere, all over Dave's turnout gear. He was slipping all over the asphalt trying to get a grip on the door frame. We ended up having to use the backup cutter from Engine 4, which is this old, heavy beast of a tool that nobody likes using. Took us almost forty-five minutes to extricate the guy, and the whole time, the truck driver is pacing around on the shoulder, screaming into his cell phone about his insurance rates. Like, buddy, maybe worry about the guy trapped under your rig first? \n\nBut yeah... what was the question? Oh, right, the scale versus the mirror. Yeah, it's the mirror. If my waist and face don't look sharp, I just can't get into that headspace to do my job right.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight that primarily drives your decision to restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, like I was saying earlier, it really is that fear of gaining weight. If I step on the scale and it's up even just one pound—like after one of those heavy station meals or if I just drank extra water—I get this tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it and people are going to look at me and think I'm soft, or just totally out of control. \n\nWhich reminds me, speaking of out of control, our new probationary guy, probie Smith, totally lost it during a training drill yesterday. We were doing a high-angle rescue simulation off the training tower, and he was supposed to be the primary rigger. Well, he completely blanked on his knots. Just froze up. The dummy we were supposed to be rescuing ended up dropping like ten feet because his friction hitch wasn't set right. Captain was screaming so loud I think the next station over heard him. Smith looked like he was about to cry, and honestly, I felt bad for the kid, but you can't mess around with that stuff. If that was a real person, they'd be dead. We had to spend the next three hours going over basic rope work in the bay while it was ninety degrees out, and we were all sweating through our station tees. \n\nBut, uh... sorry, I'm rambling again. What were we talking about? Right, restricting. Yeah, it's the thought of gaining weight that drives it, for sure. Just that panic of getting soft.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake, rather than trying to achieve a specific body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it's definitely the weight thing that gets me the most. Like I said before, if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—maybe I had too much water, or we had a heavy meal at the station—I just get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. I just feel like I've totally blown it and everyone is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I can't control myself. \n\nSpeaking of not being able to control things, our station's dalmatian, Sparky, got into the pantry yesterday. Someone left the door cracked, and that dog is way smarter than he looks. He managed to rip open a ten-pound bag of flour and dragged it through the entire day room. It looked like it snowed inside. We were finding white paw prints on the couches, the TV remote, even on Chief's boots, which he was not happy about. We spent, like, two hours trying to vacuum it all up, but every time someone walked through, another little cloud of flour puffed up from the rug. Sparky just sat there watching us, wagging his tail like he did us a favor. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right, the scale. Yeah, it's that panic of gaining weight and looking soft that really makes me restrict.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of gaining weight or the desire to look a certain way that most influences your decision to restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely the weight thing, like I've been saying. Yeah, doc, I feel like we're going in circles here a bit, but yes, it's definitely the weight. It's not really about trying to look like a fitness model or anything, it's just... if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound. But anyway... yeah, sorry. Like I was saying, if the scale goes up, I just panic. It makes me feel like I’ve completely blown it, you know? Like everyone at the house is going to look at me and think I’m getting soft or that I'm just totally out of control.
